        - Q54: Which one of the following pairs is correctly matched? [Harappan-Related]  
            - Pairs:  
                (a) The Harappan civilization — Painted Greyware  
                (b) The Kushans — Gandhara School of Art  
                (c) The Mughals — Ajanta Paintings  
                (d) The Marathas — Pahari School of Painting  
            - Answer:  
                (b) The Kushans — Gandhara School of Art  
            - Analysis:  
                - Why the Kushans and Gandhara School of Art?  
                    - The Gandhara School of Art developed during the Kushan rule (1st-3rd century CE).  
                    - It was influenced by Greco-Roman styles due to the interaction with Hellenistic cultures.  
                    - Major patron: Kanishka the Great (c. 127 - 150 CE).  
                    - Famous for Buddhist sculptures with Greco-Roman features (e.g., Buddha with wavy hair, draped robes).  
                - Why Not Others?  
                    - (a) The Harappan civilization — Painted Greyware (Incorrect)  
                        - The Harappans (2600 - 1900 BCE) used Red and Black pottery, not Painted Greyware (PGW).  
                        - PGW culture (c. 1200 - 600 BCE) is associated with the Vedic period and later settlements like Hastinapura.  
                    - (c) The Mughals — Ajanta Paintings (Incorrect)  
                        - Ajanta Caves (2nd century BCE - 6th century CE) belong to the Satavahana and Vakataka periods, not the Mughals (16th-18th century CE).  
                        - Mughals are known for miniature paintings (e.g., Akbarnama, Padshahnama).  
                    - (d) The Marathas — Pahari School of Painting (Incorrect)  
                        - The Pahari School (17th - 19th century CE) developed in Himalayan hill states (e.g., Kangra, Guler, Basohli).  
                        - The Marathas (17th - 19th century CE) were primarily associated with Deccan and Western India, not Pahari art.  
                - Key Takeaway:  
                    - The Kushans and Gandhara School of Art is the correct match, highlighting the Greco-Buddhist artistic legacy.  
        - Q55: Statements
                Assertion (A): Harshavardhana convened the Prayag Assembly.  
                Reason (R): He wanted to popularize only the Mahayana form of Buddhism.  
            - Options:  
                (a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A  
                (b) Both A and R are true but R is not a correct explanation of A  
                (c) A is true but R is false  
                (d) A is false but R is true  
            - Answer:  
                (b) Both A and R are true but R is not a correct explanation of A  
            - Analysis:  
                - Why is Assertion (A) true? 
                    - Harshavardhana (606 - 647 CE), the ruler of Thanesar and Kannauj, organized the Prayag Assembly (also called the Maha Moksha Parishad) at Prayag (modern-day Allahabad).  
                    - The assembly was held every five years at the confluence of the Ganga and Yamuna rivers.  
                    - Purpose of the Assembly:  
                        - To distribute wealth among the poor and religious institutions.  
                        - To encourage religious discussions among Hindus, Jains, and Buddhists.  
                        - To perform Dana (charity), considered a virtuous act.  
                - Why is Reason (R) false?  
                    - Harshavardhana was a follower of Mahayana Buddhism, but he was tolerant of all religions.  
                    - He did not aim to promote only Mahayana Buddhism, but supported various religious traditions, including Shaivism and Jainism.  
                    - The Chinese traveler Xuanzang (Hiuen Tsang), who attended the assembly, noted that Harsha honored all religions.  
                - Key Takeaway:  
                    - Harshavardhana convened the Prayag Assembly primarily to promote charity and religious harmony, not to propagate only Mahayana Buddhism.                  
        - Q56: Which one of the Chola kings conquered Ceylon?  
            - Options:  
                (a) Aditya-I  
                (b) Rajaraja-I  
                (c) Rajendra  
                (d) Vijayalaya  
            - Answer:  
                (c) Rajendra  
            - Analysis:  
                - Who was Rajendra Chola-I?  
                    - Rajendra Chola-I (1014 - 1044 CE), son of Rajaraja Chola-I, was one of the most powerful and successful rulers of the Chola dynasty.  
                    - He continued his father’s legacy of military expansion and cultural development, strengthening Chola rule across South India, Sri Lanka, and Southeast Asia.  
                - Rajendra's Conquest of Ceylon (Sri Lanka):  
                    - Rajendra Chola-I launched a series of military campaigns in Sri Lanka, which were particularly significant during his reign.  
                    - In the early 11th century, Rajendra defeated the local rulers of Sri Lanka and consolidated Chola control over the northern regions of the island.  
                    - Rajendra's conquest included the capture of the capital city of Anuradhapura and the establishment of Chola dominance over Sri Lanka for several decades.  
                    - He also solidified Chola naval power in the Indian Ocean, extending the empire’s influence over maritime trade routes.  
                - Why Not Others?  
                    - (a) Aditya-I (c. 870 - 907 CE):  
                        - Aditya-I, the first significant Chola ruler after Vijayalaya, was instrumental in the revival of the Chola dynasty but did not lead any conquests in Sri Lanka.  
                        - His primary focus was on consolidating Chola rule in Tamil Nadu and defeating the Pallavas.  
                    - (b) Rajaraja-I (985 - 1014 CE):  
                        - Rajaraja-I, the father of Rajendra, did indeed conquer parts of Sri Lanka, particularly the northern regions like Anuradhapura, but it was Rajendra who completed the Chola conquest and solidified their control over Ceylon.  
                    - (d) Vijayalaya Chola (c. 850 CE):  
                        - Vijayalaya Chola was the founder of the Chola dynasty and established Chola rule in Tamil Nadu after defeating the Pandyas, but he did not conduct any campaigns in Sri Lanka.  
                - Other Prominent Chola Kings:  
                    - Rajadhiraja Chola (1044 - 1054 CE):  
                        - Rajadhiraja expanded the Chola Empire through campaigns in Sri Lanka and the Deccan.  
                        - He is also known for the Battle of Koppam, where he defeated the Western Chalukyas.  
                    - Rajendra Chola II (1054 - 1063 CE):  
                        - Rajendra II was involved in several campaigns in Sri Lanka, consolidating Chola authority and defending against internal challenges.  
                    - Kulottunga Chola I (1070 - 1122 CE):  
                        - Kulottunga I was an important Chola ruler who unified the Chola and Eastern Chalukya kingdoms, strengthening Chola dominance in Southeast Asia.  
                        - His reign also marked a period of economic growth and cultural development.  
                - Key Takeaway:  
                    - Rajendra Chola-I was the Chola king who successfully conquered and consolidated Chola rule in Sri Lanka, completing his father Rajaraja's initial efforts.  
                    - Rajendra's reign marked the height of Chola influence in Sri Lanka and Southeast Asia.
                    
        - Q57: Who among the following presided over the Buddhist Council held during the reign of Kanishka at Kashmir?  
            - Options:  
                (a) Parsva  
                (b) Nagarjuna  
                (c) Sudraka  
                (d) Vasumitra  
            - Answer:  
                (d) Vasumitra  
            - Analysis:  
                - Historical Context:  
                    - Kanishka, the most prominent Kushana ruler (c. 127 – 150 CE), was a great patron of Buddhism.  
                    - He convened the Fourth Buddhist Council around 78 – 101 CE in Kundalvana (Kashmir).  
                    - This council was instrumental in the development of the Mahayana sect of Buddhism.  
                - Role of Vasumitra:  
                    - Vasumitra, a renowned Buddhist scholar, presided over the council.  
                    - Ashvaghosha, another major Buddhist thinker, played a key role in the discussions.  
                    - The council aimed to compile and systematize Buddhist scriptures, leading to a clearer division between Mahayana and Hinayana Buddhism.  
                - Why Not Others?  
                    - (a) Parsva:  
                        - Parsva was a Jain Tirthankara, not a Buddhist scholar, and was not associated with Buddhist councils.  
                    - (b) Nagarjuna:  
                        - Nagarjuna was a famous Mahayana Buddhist philosopher, but he was active after the council.  
                    - (c) Sudraka:  
                        - Sudraka is not historically linked to Buddhist councils.  
                - Important Buddhist Councils:  
                    - First Buddhist Council (c. 483 BCE, Rajagriha)  
                        - Organizer: Mahakashyapa (after Buddha’s Mahaparinirvana)  
                        - Patron: Ajatashatru (Haryanka dynasty)  
                        - Outcome: Compilation of Vinaya Pitaka and Sutta Pitaka (earliest Buddhist scriptures).  
                    - Second Buddhist Council (c. 383 BCE, Vaishali)  
                        - Organizer: Sabakami  
                        - Patron: Kalashoka (Shishunaga dynasty)  
                        - Outcome: Split between Sthaviravadins (Theravada) and Mahasanghikas over monastic rules.  
                    - Third Buddhist Council (c. 250 BCE, Pataliputra)  
                        - Organizer: Moggaliputta Tissa  
                        - Patron: Emperor Ashoka (Maurya dynasty)  
                        - Outcome: Compilation of Abhidhamma Pitaka, spread of Buddhism to Sri Lanka, Burma, and other regions.  
                    - Fourth Buddhist Council (c. 78 – 101 CE, Kundalvana, Kashmir)  
                        - Organizer: Vasumitra  
                        - Patron: Kanishka (Kushana dynasty)  
                        - Outcome: Formal division of Mahayana and Hinayana Buddhism, Sanskrit used for Buddhist texts.  
                - Additional Notes:  
                    - Ashoka’s role in the Third Buddhist Council led to the spread of Buddhism outside India.  
                    - The Fourth Council solidified Mahayana Buddhism, which later spread to China, Korea, and Japan.  
                    - These councils were crucial in shaping Buddhist philosophy, scriptures, and sectarian divisions.  
                - Key Takeaway:  
                    - The Fourth Buddhist Council marked the consolidation of Mahayana Buddhism.  
                    - Kanishka’s patronage significantly contributed to the spread of Buddhism to Central Asia and China.
                
        - Q58: Which one of the following animals was not represented on seals and terracotta art of the Harappan culture? [Harappan-Related]
            - Options:
                (a) Cow
                (b) Elephant
                (c) Rhinoceros
                (d) Tiger
            - Answer:
                (a) Cow
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - The Harappan culture (c. 2600-1900 BCE), part of the Indus Valley Civilization, is known for its urban settlements (e.g., Harappa, Mohenjo-Daro) and artistic expressions, including seals and terracotta figurines. These artifacts often depict animals, reflecting their environment and possibly symbolic roles.
                - Correct Option Explained:
                    - (a) Cow:
                        - Correct—Despite cattle being domesticated and economically vital (e.g., bones found at sites), the cow is conspicuously absent from Harappan seals and terracotta art, unlike other animals. This contrasts with later Vedic reverence for the cow.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (b) Elephant:
                        - Incorrect—Elephants appear on seals (e.g., Mohenjo-Daro finds), depicted with trunks, suggesting their presence in the region (modern Gujarat, Punjab).
                    - (c) Rhinoceros:
                        - Incorrect—Rhinoceros images are rare but present on seals (e.g., a famous one-horned rhino seal from Harappa), indicating their habitat in the Indus plains.
                    - (d) Tiger:
                        - Incorrect—Tigers are depicted on seals (e.g., Harappa), reflecting the fauna of the northwest forests or symbolic significance.
                - Animals in Harappan Art:
                    - Common Depictions:
                        - Bull (humped cattle), elephant, rhinoceros, tiger, buffalo, and mythical creatures (e.g., unicorn) dominate seals.
                    - Terracotta:
                        - Includes bulls, dogs, and birds, but no cows.
                    - Absence of Cow:
                        - Possibly cultural—cows were utilitarian (milk, labor) but not symbolic on seals, unlike bulls linked to strength or fertility.
                - Archaeological Evidence:
                    - Seals:
                        - Over 2,000 found (e.g., Lothal, Gujarat; Dholavira, Gujarat), featuring elephants, rhinos, tigers, but no cows.
                    - Terracotta:
                        - Figurines from Kalibangan (Rajasthan) and Banawali (Haryana) show bulls, not cows.
                    - Cattle Bones:
                        - Abundant at sites (e.g., Harappa, Punjab), proving their domestication, yet artistic exclusion.
                - Related Figures:
                    - John Marshall:
                        - Archaeologist (1920s), led Harappan excavations, noted animal motifs on seals (e.g., Q1 [1995]).
                    - Mortimer Wheeler:
                        - Excavator (1940s), refined Harappan chronology, observed seal iconography.
                    - Ashoka:
                        - Mauryan emperor (r. 268-232 BCE), later cow protection (Edict 1), contrasting Harappan art (e.g., Q33 [1998]).
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on Harappan urbanism (e.g., Q1 [1995]), trade (e.g., Q37 [1998] on Lothal), or later cultural shifts (e.g., Q35 [1998] on urban culture).
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - The cow, though domesticated, was not represented on Harappan seals or terracotta, unlike elephants, rhinoceroses, and tigers, highlighting a unique artistic choice in the Indus Valley Civilization.

    - [2002]
        - Q59: Match List-I (Ancient site) with List-II (Archaeological finding) and select the correct answer using the codes given below the lists: [Harappan-Related]
            - List-I (Ancient site):
                A. Lothal
                B. Kalibangan
                C. Dholavira
                D. Banawali
            - List-II (Archaeological finding):
                1. Ploughed field
                2. Dockyard
                3. Terracotta replica of a plough
                4. An inscription comprising ten large sized signs of the Harappan script
            - Options:
                (a) A-1, B-2, C-4, D-3
                (b) A-2, B-1, C-4, D-3
                (c) A-1, B-2, C-3, D-4
                (d) A-2, B-1, C-3, D-4
            - Answer:
                (b) A-2, B-1, C-4, D-3
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - The Harappan culture (c. 2600-1900 BCE), part of the Indus Valley Civilization, left diverse archaeological remains across sites in modern India and Pakistan. This question tests knowledge of key findings at major sites.
                - Correct Matches Explained:
                    - A. Lothal - 2. Dockyard:
                        - Lothal (Gujarat) is famed for its dockyard, possibly the world’s earliest, linked to maritime trade with Mesopotamia (c. 2400 BCE) (e.g., Q37 [1998]).
                    - B. Kalibangan - 1. Ploughed field:
                        - Kalibangan (Rajasthan) revealed a ploughed field with furrows, one of the earliest evidence of agriculture (c. 2500 BCE).
                    - C. Dholavira - 4. An inscription comprising ten large sized signs of the Harappan script:
                        - Dholavira (Gujarat) features a unique signboard with ten large Harappan script characters, suggesting public display (c. 2500 BCE).
                    - D. Banawali - 3. Terracotta replica of a plough:
                        - Banawali (Haryana) yielded a terracotta plough model, indicating agricultural tools (c. 2300 BCE).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) A-1, B-2, C-4, D-3:
                        - Incorrect—Lothal with ploughed field (1) is wrong; its dockyard (2) is iconic, and Kalibangan’s ploughed field (1) is misplaced as dockyard (2).
                    - (c) A-1, B-2, C-3, D-4:
                        - Incorrect—Lothal as ploughed field (1) and Kalibangan as dockyard (2) are swapped; Dholavira’s plough (3) and Banawali’s inscription (4) don’t fit.
                    - (d) A-2, B-1, C-3, D-4:
                        - Incorrect—Dholavira with plough (3) and Banawali with inscription (4) are reversed; Dholavira’s signboard (4) and Banawali’s plough (3) are specific.
                - Significance of Findings:
                    - Dockyard (Lothal):
                        - Maritime trade hub, brick-lined structure (Gujarat).
                    - Ploughed Field (Kalibangan):
                        - Early farming evidence, grid-patterned furrows (Rajasthan).
                    - Inscription (Dholavira):
                        - Largest Harappan script example, undeciphered (Gujarat).
                    - Terracotta Plough (Banawali):
                        - Miniature model, agricultural insight (Haryana).
                - Archaeological Context:
                    - Lothal:
                        - Coastal site, trade-focused (Junagadh district, Gujarat).
                    - Kalibangan:
                        - Inland, agricultural evidence (Hanumangarh, Rajasthan).
                    - Dholavira:
                        - Arid island site, urban planning (Kutch, Gujarat).
                    - Banawali:
                        - Rural settlement, farming tools (Fatehabad, Haryana).
                - Other Notable Harappan Sites and Findings:
                    - Harappa:
                        - Granaries and Great Bath, early urban center (Sahiwal, Punjab, Pakistan).
                    - Mohenjo-Daro:
                        - Great Bath and seal with "Pashupati" figure, advanced drainage (Larkana, Sindh, Pakistan).
                    - Rakhigarhi:
                        - Largest Harappan site, cemetery with burials (Hisar, Haryana).
                    - Surkotada:
                        - Horse remains (disputed), fortified citadel (Kutch, Gujarat).
                    - Chanhudaro:
                        - Bead-making workshop, no citadel (Nawabshah, Sindh, Pakistan).
                    - Ropar (Rupar):
                        - Dog buried with human, early Harappan evidence (Rupnagar, Punjab, India).
                - Related Figures:
                    - John Marshall:
                        - Archaeologist (1920s), oversaw early Harappan digs (e.g., Q1 [1995]).
                    - Mortimer Wheeler:
                        - Excavator (1940s), excavated Lothal, Kalibangan, noted dockyard (e.g., Q58 [1998]).
                    - S.R. Rao:
                        - Archaeologist (1950s-60s), excavated Lothal, confirmed dockyard.
                    - B.B. Lal:
                        - Excavated Kalibangan, Banawali, identified ploughed field, plough.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to Harappan trade (e.g., Q37 [1998] on Lothal), agriculture (e.g., Q35 [1998] on iron plough context), or script mysteries (e.g., Q58 [1998]).
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Correct matches (Lothal-Dockyard, Kalibangan-Ploughed field, Dholavira-Inscription, Banawali-Terracotta plough) reflect diverse Harappan advancements in trade, farming, and writing.
        - Q60: Which one of the following statements is not correct?
            - Options:
                (a) The statue of Gomateshwara at Shravana Belagola represents the last Tirthankara of Jains
                (b) India's largest Buddhist monastery is in Arunachal Pradesh
                (c) Khajuraho temples were built under Chandela kings
                (d) Hoysaleswara temple is dedicated to Siva
            - Answer:
                (a) The statue of Gomateshwara at Shravana Belagola represents the last Tirthankara of Jains
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - This question spans Jain, Buddhist, and Hindu heritage across India, from ancient to medieval periods (c. 3rd century BCE to 13th century CE), testing accuracy of cultural and architectural facts.
                - Evaluation of Statements:
                    - (a) The statue of Gomateshwara at Shravana Belagola represents the last Tirthankara of Jains:
                        - Incorrect—Gomateshwara (Bahubali) at Shravana Belagola (Karnataka), built c. 983 CE under the Ganga dynasty, is a Jain figure but not a Tirthankara. He was the son of the first Tirthankara, Rishabhanatha. The last (24th) Tirthankara is Mahavira (c. 6th century BCE).
                    - (b) India's largest Buddhist monastery is in Arunachal Pradesh:
                        - Correct—Tawang Monastery in Arunachal Pradesh, founded c. 17th century CE, is India’s largest Buddhist monastery, though this is a later period beyond ancient times.
                    - (c) Khajuraho temples were built under Chandela kings:
                        - Correct—Khajuraho temples (Madhya Pradesh), built c. 9th-11th centuries CE under the Chandela dynasty, are renowned for Hindu and Jain architecture (e.g., Kandariya Mahadeva).
                    - (d) Hoysaleswara temple is dedicated to Siva:
                        - Correct—Hoysaleswara temple (Halebidu, Karnataka), built c. 12th century CE under the Hoysala dynasty, is a Shiva temple known for its intricate carvings.
                - Why (a) is Incorrect:
                    - Bahubali (Gomateshwara) was a Jain prince who renounced his kingdom, achieving liberation, but he’s not among the 24 Tirthankaras. The statue symbolizes his penance, not Tirthankara status, unlike Mahavira, the last Tirthankara.
                - Details of Correct Statements:
                    - Tawang Monastery:
                        - Largest in India, 17th-century Buddhist site (Arunachal Pradesh).
                    - Khajuraho Temples:
                        - Chandela patronage, Hindu-Jain mix (Chhatarpur, Madhya Pradesh).
                    - Hoysaleswara Temple:
                        - Hoysala Shiva temple, architectural peak (Hassan, Karnataka).
                - Jain Tirthankaras Context:
                    - 24 Tirthankaras, from Rishabhanatha (1st) to Mahavira (24th), are spiritual teachers. Bahubali, though revered, is distinct as a Kevalin (liberated soul), not a Tirthankara.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Mahavira:
                        - 24th Jain Tirthankara (c. 6th century BCE), last in the lineage (e.g., Q32 [1998] context).
                    - Ganga Dynasty:
                        - Chavundaraya, minister (c. 983 CE), commissioned Gomateshwara statue.
                    - Chandela Kings:
                        - Yashovarman, Dhanga (9th-11th centuries CE), built Khajuraho temples.
                    - Hoysala Kings:
                        - Vishnuvardhana (r. 1108-1152 CE), patron of Hoysaleswara temple.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to Jainism (e.g., Q32 [1998] on Buddhism contrast), temple architecture (e.g., Q37 [1998] on Sarnath), or regional dynasties (e.g., Q40 [1998] on empires).
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - The Gomateshwara statue at Shravana Belagola represents Bahubali, not the last Tirthankara (Mahavira), making (a) incorrect, while Buddhist, Chandela, and Hoysala statements hold true.
        - Q61: In ancient Indian Buddhist monasteries, a ceremony called Pavarana used to be held. It was the:
            - Options:
                (a) occasion to elect the Sanghparinayaka and two speakers one on Dhamma and the other on Vinaya
                (b) confession by monks of their offences committed during their stay in the monasteries during the rainy season
                (c) ceremony of initiation of new person into the Buddhist Sangha in which the head is shaved and yellow robes are offered
                (d) gathering of Buddhist monks on the next day to the full-moon day of Ashadha when they take up a fixed abode for the next four months of the rainy season
            - Answer:
                (b) confession by monks of their offences committed during their stay in the monasteries during the rainy season
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Buddhist monastic traditions, rooted in the 5th century BCE with Gautama Buddha, developed rituals like Pavarana to maintain discipline and community harmony within the Sangha, especially during the rainy season retreat (Vassavasa).
                - Correct Option Explained:
                    - (b) confession by monks of their offences committed during their stay in the monasteries during the rainy season:
                        - Correct—Pavarana, held at the end of Vassavasa (rainy season retreat, typically July-October), was a ceremony where monks confessed any breaches of monastic rules (Vinaya) to each other, seeking forgiveness to restore harmony. It traditionally occurs on the full-moon day of Kartika (October-November).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) occasion to elect the Sanghparinayaka and two speakers one on Dhamma and the other on Vinaya:
                        - Incorrect—No evidence in Buddhist texts (e.g., Vinaya Pitaka) links Pavarana to elections; it’s about confession, not leadership selection.
                    - (c) ceremony of initiation of new person into the Buddhist Sangha in which the head is shaved and yellow robes are offered:
                        - Incorrect—This describes Upasampada (ordination), not Pavarana, which is a post-retreat ritual for existing monks.
                    - (d) gathering of Buddhist monks on the next day to the full-moon day of Ashadha when they take up a fixed abode for the next four months of the rainy season:
                        - Incorrect—This refers to the start of Vassavasa (on Ashadha full-moon, June-July), not Pavarana, which marks its end.
                - Buddhist Monastic Rituals:
                    - Vassavasa:
                        - Rainy season retreat (3-4 months), begins Ashadha full-moon, monks stay in fixed abodes (e.g., Nalanda, Bihar).
                    - Pavarana:
                        - End of retreat, Kartika full-moon, mutual confession and reconciliation.
                    - Uposatha:
                        - Bi-monthly recitation of Patimokkha (rules), distinct from Pavarana’s annual focus.
                - Purpose of Pavarana:
                    - Ensures Sangha unity by addressing offences (e.g., minor rule violations) openly, fostering trust after months of close confinement during rains.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Gautama Buddha:
                        - Founder (c. 5th century BCE), established Pavarana and Vassavasa (e.g., Q39 [1998]).
                    - Ashoka:
                        - Mauryan emperor (r. 268-232 BCE), supported monasteries where such rituals thrived (e.g., Q33 [1998]).
                    - Hieun-Tsang:
                        - Chinese pilgrim (c. 629-645 CE), described monastic life, including retreat practices (e.g., Q42 [1998]).
                    - Mahinda:
                        - Ashoka’s son (c. 3rd century BCE), spread Buddhism to Sri Lanka, where Pavarana continued.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to Buddhist practices (e.g., Q39 [1998] on Eight-fold Path), monastic sites (e.g., Q37 [1998] on Sarnath), or travelers’ accounts (e.g., Q42 [1998]).
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Pavarana was the end-of-retreat confession ceremony for monks, distinct from initiation, elections, or the start of Vassavasa, emphasizing Sangha discipline.
        - Q62: The ancient Indian play Mudrarakshasa of Vishakhadatta has its subject on:
            - Options:
                (a) a conflict between Gods and Demons of ancient Hindu lore
                (b) a romantic story of an Aryan prince and a tribal woman
                (c) the story of the power struggle between two Aryan tribes
                (d) the court intrigues at the time of Chandragupta
            - Answer:
                (d) the court intrigues at the time of Chandragupta
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Mudrarakshasa, a Sanskrit play by Vishakhadatta (c. 4th-5th century CE, Gupta period), is a political drama reflecting the Mauryan era (c. 321-297 BCE). It showcases intrigue and strategy, a hallmark of ancient Indian literature.
                - Correct Option Explained:
                    - (d) the court intrigues at the time of Chandragupta:
                        - Correct—The play centers on the political maneuvers of Chanakya (Kautilya) to secure Chandragupta Maurya’s throne against rivals, notably Rakshasa, the last Nanda minister. It dramatizes the overthrow of the Nanda dynasty and the establishment of Mauryan rule through cunning diplomacy (e.g., forging alliances, using spies).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) a conflict between Gods and Demons of ancient Hindu lore:
                        - Incorrect—This theme fits mythological epics like the Mahabharata, not Mudrarakshasa, which is historical and political.
                    - (b) a romantic story of an Aryan prince and a tribal woman:
                        - Incorrect—No romance drives the plot; it’s about power, not love, unlike plays like Kalidasa’s Abhijnanashakuntalam.
                    - (c) the story of the power struggle between two Aryan tribes:
                        - Incorrect—It’s not a tribal conflict but a dynastic struggle between the Nandas and Mauryas, centered at Pataliputra (modern Patna, Bihar).
                - Plot Summary:
                    - Chanakya, Chandragupta’s advisor, uses a signet ring (mudra) to outwit Rakshasa, turning him into an ally. The play highlights statecraft, not mythology or romance, aligning with Kautilya’s Arthashastra principles.
                - Literary Significance:
                    - Genre:
                        - Nataka (heroic drama), focusing on historical figures and political strategy.
                    - Style:
                        - Sanskrit prose and verse, with intrigue as the central theme.
                    - Period:
                        - Written later (Gupta era), but set in the Mauryan founding (c. 321 BCE).
                - Other Notable Ancient Indian Plays:
                    - Mrichchakatikam (The Little Clay Cart) - Shudraka:
                        - c. 4th-5th century CE (Gupta period), a social drama blending romance and realism, about a Brahmin and a courtesan (e.g., Q34 [1998]).
                    - Abhijnanashakuntalam (The Recognition of Shakuntala) - Kalidasa:
                        - c. 4th-5th century CE (Gupta period), a romantic play about King Dushyanta and Shakuntala, blending love and mythology.
                    - Malavikagnimitram - Kalidasa:
                        - c. 4th-5th century CE, a romantic drama about King Agnimitra (Shunga dynasty) and Malavika, with courtly intrigue.
                    - Ratnavali - Harsha:
                        - c. 7th century CE, a romantic play by King Harsha, about Princess Ratnavali and King Udayana.
                    - Swapnavasavadatta - Bhasa:
                        - c. 2nd century BCE (pre-Mauryan or early Mauryan), a romantic drama about King Udayana and Vasavadatta, one of 13 plays attributed to Bhasa.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Vishakhadatta:
                        - Playwright (c. 4th-5th century CE), also linked to fragments of Devi-Chandraguptam (e.g., Q34 [1998]).
                    - Chandragupta Maurya:
                        - Mauryan founder (r. 321-297 BCE), protagonist of the play (e.g., Q42 [1998]).
                    - Chanakya (Kautilya):
                        - Mauryan advisor, mastermind in Mudrarakshasa, authored Arthashastra.
                    - Megasthanese:
                        - Greek envoy (c. 302-298 BCE), documented Mauryan court, contemporary context (e.g., Q42 [1998]).
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to Mauryan history (e.g., Q34 [1998] on literature), political strategy (e.g., Q38 [1998] on records), or Gupta-era works (e.g., Q41 [1998]).
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Mudrarakshasa by Vishakhadatta portrays court intrigues during Chandragupta Maurya’s rise, focusing on Chanakya’s political genius, not myths, romance, or tribal wars.
        - Q63: Emperor Harsha's southward march was stopped on the Narmada river by:
            - Options:
                (a) Pulakesin-I
                (b) Pulakesin-II
                (c) Vikramaditya-I
                (d) Vikramaditya-II
            - Answer:
                (b) Pulakesin-II
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Harsha (r. 606-647 CE), ruler of Thaneswar and later Kanauj, sought to expand his North Indian empire southward. This event, c. 620 CE, marks a key clash with the Chalukyas of Badami, a dominant Deccan power.
                - Correct Option Explained:
                    - (b) Pulakesin-II:
                        - Correct—Pulakesin-II (r. 610-642 CE), Chalukya king of Vatapi (modern Badami, Karnataka), defeated Harsha on the Narmada river (c. 620 CE), halting his southern expansion. This is recorded in Pulakesin-II’s Aihole inscription (634 CE) and corroborated by Hieun-Tsang’s account of Harsha’s limits.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Pulakesin-I:
                        - Incorrect—Pulakesin-I (r. 543-566 CE), founder of the Chalukya dynasty, predates Harsha by decades and died before this conflict.
                    - (c) Vikramaditya-I:
                        - Incorrect—Vikramaditya-I (r. 655-680 CE), a later Chalukya king, succeeded Pulakesin-II and fought the Pallavas, not Harsha, who died in 647 CE.
                    - (d) Vikramaditya-II:
                        - Incorrect—Vikramaditya-II (r. 733-746 CE), another Chalukya ruler, lived a century after Harsha, clashing with Pallavas, not Harsha.
                - Event Details:
                    - Harsha’s Campaign:
                        - After unifying North India (e.g., Kanauj, modern Uttar Pradesh), Harsha marched south, reaching the Narmada (modern Madhya Pradesh-Maharashtra border).
                    - Pulakesin-II’s Victory:
                        - Chalukya forces repelled Harsha, preserving Deccan autonomy. The Aihole inscription boasts Pulakesin-II’s triumph over Harsha’s "innumerable army."
                - Historical Evidence:
                    - Aihole Inscription:
                        - Sanskrit eulogy (634 CE) by Ravikirti, claims Pulakesin-II made Harsha’s "war-elephants retreat" (Badami, Karnataka).
                    - Hieun-Tsang:
                        - Chinese pilgrim (c. 629-645 CE) notes Harsha’s power stopped at the Narmada, implying a military check.
                - Notable Chalukya Rulers: Pulakesins and Vikramadityas:
                    - Western Chalukyas of Badami:
                        - Pulakesin-I:
                            - r. 543-566 CE, founded the dynasty at Vatapi (Badami, Karnataka).
                        - Pulakesin-II:
                            - r. 610-642 CE, defeated Harsha (c. 620 CE), Aihole inscription (Badami, Karnataka).
                        - Vikramaditya-I:
                            - r. 655-680 CE, recaptured Vatapi from Pallavas after their sack in 642 CE.
                        - Vikramaditya-II:
                            - r. 733-746 CE, defeated Pallavas, sacked Kanchi (Tamil Nadu).
                    - Eastern Chalukyas of Vengi:
                        - Pulakesin (Pulikesi):
                            - c. 7th century CE, lesser-known ruler in the Vengi branch (Andhra Pradesh), postdates Harsha.
                    - Later Chalukyas of Kalyani:
                        - Vikramaditya-VI:
                            - r. 1076-1126 CE, famous for Chalukya resurgence, patron of Bilhana’s Vikramankadevacharita (Kalyani, Karnataka).
                    - Other Dynasties:
                        - Vikramaditya (Chandragupta II):
                            - Gupta king (r. 375-415 CE), took the title "Vikramaditya," predates Chalukyas (e.g., Mehrauli Pillar, Delhi).
                - Related Figures:
                    - Harsha:
                        - Emperor (r. 606-647 CE), North Indian ruler, patron of Hieun-Tsang (e.g., Q42 [1998]).
                    - Pulakesin-II:
                        - Chalukya king (r. 610-642 CE), victor at Narmada, Aihole inscription author.
                    - Hieun-Tsang:
                        - Traveler (c. 629-645 CE), documented Harsha’s reign and limits (e.g., Q42 [1998]).
                    - Chandragupta II:
                        - Gupta king (r. 375-415 CE), earlier southern reach, contrast to Harsha (e.g., Q38 [1998]).
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to Harsha’s reign (e.g., Q40 [1998]), Chalukya power (e.g., Q60 [1998] context), or travelers’ accounts (e.g., Q42 [1998]).
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Pulakesin-II stopped Harsha’s southward march at the Narmada (c. 620 CE), a pivotal moment marking the Chalukya-Mauryan divide, unlike the unrelated reigns of Pulakesin-I or Vikramadityas.

        - Q64: Consider the following statements:
            - Statements:
                1. The Cholas defeated Pandya and Chera rulers and established their domination over peninsular India in the early medieval times
                2. The Cholas sent an expedition against Sailendra empire of South-East Asia and conquered some of the areas
            - Options:
                (a) Only 1
                (b) Only 2
                (c) Both 1 and 2
                (d) Neither 1 nor 2
            - Answer:
                (c) Both 1 and 2
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - The Chola dynasty (c. 9th-13th centuries CE), based in Tamil Nadu, rose as a dominant power in South India during the early medieval period (c. 600-1200 CE), excelling in warfare, trade, and maritime expansion.
                - Evaluation of Statements:
                    - 1. The Cholas defeated Pandya and Chera rulers and established their domination over peninsular India in the early medieval times:
                        - True—Under Rajaraja I (r. 985-1014 CE) and Rajendra I (r. 1012-1044 CE), the Cholas defeated the Pandyas (Madurai region) and Cheras (Kerala coast) by the early 11th century, dominating peninsular India (modern Tamil Nadu, Kerala, parts of Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh). This is evident from inscriptions like the Thiruvalangadu plates.
                    - 2. The Cholas sent an expedition against Sailendra empire of South-East Asia and conquered some of the areas:
                        - True—In 1025 CE, Rajendra I launched a naval expedition against the Sailendra empire (Srivijaya, modern Indonesia, Malaysia), capturing areas like Kedah and parts of Sumatra. The Tanjore inscription (c. 1030 CE) lists these conquests, reflecting Chola maritime prowess.
                - Why Both Are Correct?:
                    - Statement 1:
                        - Chola victories over Pandyas (e.g., Battle of Velur, c. 985 CE) and Cheras (e.g., Rajaraja’s campaigns) solidified their control over South India by the 11th century, a peak of early medieval power.
                    - Statement 2:
                        - The 1025 CE expedition targeted Sailendra ports to secure trade routes (e.g., Malacca Straits), with conquests like Kadaram (Kedah) noted in Chola records, showing overseas influence.
                - Chola Achievements:
                    - Peninsular Domination:
                        - Unified Tamil Nadu, subdued Pandyas and Cheras, built temples (e.g., Brihadeeswara, Thanjavur).
                    - Maritime Expansion:
                        - Naval raids on Srivijaya, control over Bay of Bengal trade, cultural spread (e.g., Hindu temples in Kedah).
                - Evidence:
                    - Thiruvalangadu Plates:
                        - Copper-plate grant (c. 1018 CE) details Rajendra I’s victories over Pandyas and Cheras (Thanjavur, Tamil Nadu).
                    - Tanjore Inscription:
                        - Rajendra I’s record (c. 1030 CE) lists South-East Asian conquests (Gangaikonda Cholapuram, Tamil Nadu).
                - Related Figures:
                    - Rajaraja I:
                        - Chola king (r. 985-1014 CE), began Pandya-Chera conquests, built Brihadeeswara temple.
                    - Rajendra I:
                        - Chola king (r. 1012-1044 CE), completed South Indian domination, led Sailendra expedition.
                    - Harsha:
                        - North Indian ruler (r. 606-647 CE), earlier peninsular limit (e.g., Q63 [1998]).
                    - Ashoka:
                        - Mauryan emperor (r. 268-232 BCE), earlier South Indian ties (e.g., Q33 [1998]).
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to Chola power (e.g., Q63 [1998] context), maritime trade (e.g., Q41 [1998] on South-East Asia), or South Indian history (e.g., Q40 [1998]).
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Both statements are correct: the Cholas dominated peninsular India by defeating Pandyas and Cheras (Statement 1) and conquered Sailendra regions in South-East Asia (Statement 2) under Rajaraja I and Rajendra I.
        - Q65: ‘Mrichchhakatika' an ancient Indian book written by Shudraka deals with:
            - Options:
                (a) the love affair of a rich merchant with the daughter of a courtesan
                (b) the victory of Chandragupta II over the Shaka Kshatrapas of western India
                (c) the military expeditions and exploits of Samudragupta
                (d) the love affairs between a Gupta king and a princess of Kamarupa
            - Answer:
                (a) the love affair of a rich merchant with the daughter of a courtesan
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Mrichchhakatika (The Little Clay Cart), a Sanskrit play by Shudraka (c. 4th-5th century CE, Gupta period), is a notable work of ancient Indian literature, blending romance, social commentary, and drama from the post-Mauryan or Gupta era.
                - Correct Option Explained:
                    - (a) the love affair of a rich merchant with the daughter of a courtesan:
                        - Correct—The play revolves around Charudatta, a poor but noble merchant, and his love for Vasantasena, a courtesan’s daughter, set against a backdrop of intrigue and a political uprising in Ujjain (modern Madhya Pradesh). It’s a human story, not a royal epic.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (b) the victory of Chandragupta II over the Shaka Kshatrapas of western India:
                        - Incorrect—This historical event (c. 400 CE) is linked to the Mehrauli Pillar inscription, not Mrichchhakatika, which is fictional and lacks military focus.
                    - (c) the military expeditions and exploits of Samudragupta:
                        - Incorrect—Samudragupta’s campaigns (c. 335-375 CE) are detailed in the Prayaga-prasasti, not this play, which avoids martial themes.
                    - (d) the love affairs between a Gupta king and a princess of Kamarupa:
                        - Incorrect—No Gupta king or Kamarupa (Assam) princess features; the play’s characters are commoners and courtesans, not royalty.
                - Plot Summary:
                    - Charudatta, once wealthy, loves Vasantasena, who leaves her courtesan life for him. A subplot involves a thief and a revolt against a tyrant, Palaka, blending romance with social tension. The title refers to a child’s toy cart pivotal to the climax.
                - Literary Significance:
                    - Genre:
                        - Prakarana (social drama), distinct from heroic Natakas like Mudrarakshasa.
                    - Themes:
                        - Love, poverty, justice, and urban life in ancient India.
                    - Period:
                        - Likely Gupta era (c. 4th-5th century CE), reflecting a cosmopolitan society.
                - Other Similar Ancient Indian Plays:
                    - Mudrarakshasa - Vishakhadatta:
                        - c. 4th-5th century CE (Gupta period), political drama about Chanakya and Chandragupta Maurya’s court intrigues (e.g., Q62 [1998]).
                    - Abhijnanashakuntalam - Kalidasa:
                        - c. 4th-5th century CE (Gupta period), romantic play about King Dushyanta and Shakuntala, blending love and mythology.
                    - Malavikagnimitram - Kalidasa:
                        - c. 4th-5th century CE, romantic drama about King Agnimitra (Shunga dynasty) and Malavika, with courtly intrigue.
                    - Ratnavali - Harsha:
                        - c. 7th century CE, romantic play by King Harsha about Princess Ratnavali and King Udayana.
                    - Swapnavasavadatta - Bhasa:
                        - c. 2nd century BCE (pre-Mauryan or early Mauryan), romantic drama about King Udayana and Vasavadatta, one of Bhasa’s 13 plays.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Shudraka:
                        - Playwright (c. 4th-5th century CE), attributed author, possibly a king or poet (e.g., Q34 [1998]).
                    - Kalidasa:
                        - Gupta-era poet (c. 4th-5th century CE), wrote romantic plays (e.g., Abhijnanashakuntalam), contemporary of Shudraka.
                    - Vishakhadatta:
                        - Author of Mudrarakshasa (c. 4th-5th century CE), contrast with political focus (e.g., Q62 [1998]).
                    - Chandragupta II:
                        - Gupta king (r. 375-415 CE), unrelated but from the play’s possible era (e.g., Q38 [1998]).
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to Gupta literature (e.g., Q34 [1998]), social themes (e.g., Q62 [1998] on Mudrarakshasa contrast), or urban culture (e.g., Q35 [1998]).
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Mrichchhakatika by Shudraka narrates the love story of a merchant and a courtesan’s daughter, not royal victories or expeditions, showcasing everyday life in ancient India.
        - Q66: Consider the following statements:
            - Statements:
                1. Vardhaman Mahavira's mother was the daughter of Lichchhavi chief Chetaka
                2. Gautama Buddha's mother was a princess from the Koshalan dynasty
                3. Parshvanatha, the twenty-third Tirthankara, belonged to Benaras
            - Options:
                (a) Only 1
                (b) Only 2
                (c) 2 and 3
                (d) 1, 2 and 3
            - Answer:
                (c) 2 and 3
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - This question examines key figures of Jainism and Buddhism from the 6th-5th centuries BCE, a period of spiritual ferment in ancient India’s Gangetic plains, involving the Lichchhavis, Koshalans, and Kashi (Benaras).
                - Evaluation of Statements:
                    - 1. Vardhaman Mahavira's mother was the daughter of Lichchhavi chief Chetaka:
                        - False — Mahavira (c. 599-527 BCE), the 24th Jain Tirthankara, was born to Trishala (or Trishaladevi), however she was not dauther but sister of Chetaka, a powerful Lichchhavi chief of Vaishali (modern Bihar). Jain texts (e.g., Kalpa Sutra) confirm this lineage.
                    - 2. Gautama Buddha's mother was a princess from the Koshalan dynasty:
                        - True — Gautama Buddha (c. 563-483 BCE) was born to Maya (Mayadevi), a princess of the Koliya clan, closely tied to the Koshalan (Kosala) dynasty through her marriage to Suddhodana, a Shakya chief under Kosala’s influence (modern Uttar Pradesh). Buddhist texts (e.g., Mahavastu) support this.
                    - 3. Parshvanatha, the twenty-third Tirthankara, belonged to Benaras:
                        - True — Parshvanatha (c. 877-777 BCE), the 23rd Jain Tirthankara, was born in Varanasi (Benaras, modern Uttar Pradesh) to King Ashvasena and Queen Vama of the Ikshvaku dynasty, as per Jain tradition (e.g., Uttarapurana).
                - Why only 2 and 3 are Correct?:
                    - Statement 1:
                        - Trishala’s connection to Chetaka ties Mahavira to the Lichchhavi republic, a key political entity in Magadha. But she was not daughter of Chetaka, but sister
                    - Statement 2:
                        - Maya’s Koliya origin aligns with Kosala’s regional dominance, though the Shakyas were vassals; "Koshalan dynasty" is broadly accurate in this context.
                    - Statement 3:
                        - Parshvanatha’s birth in Benaras (Kashi) predates Mahavira by centuries, marking an early Jain presence there.
                - Biographical Details:
                    - Mahavira:
                        - Born in Kundagrama (near Vaishali, Bihar), mother Trishala, father Siddhartha of the Jnatrika clan.
                    - Buddha:
                        - Born in Lumbini (modern Nepal), mother Maya, father Suddhodana of the Shakya clan under Kosala.
                    - Parshvanatha:
                        - Born in Varanasi (Uttar Pradesh), preached non-violence, influenced Mahavira’s teachings.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Vardhaman Mahavira:
                        - 24th Tirthankara (c. 599-527 BCE), founded modern Jainism (e.g., Q60 [1998]).
                    - Gautama Buddha:
                        - Founder of Buddhism (c. 563-483 BCE), First Sermon at Sarnath (e.g., Q39 [1998]).
                    - Parshvanatha:
                        - 23rd Tirthankara (c. 877-777 BCE), early Jain leader, Benaras origin.
                    - Chetaka:
                        - Lichchhavi chief (c. 6th century BCE), Mahavira’s grandfather, allied with Magadha.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to Jainism (e.g., Q60 [1998]), Buddhism (e.g., Q39 [1998]), or Gangetic kingdoms (e.g., Q42 [1998] on travelers).
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - All statements are correct: Mahavira’s mother was Chetaka’s daughter, Buddha’s mother was a Koshalan princess, and Parshvanatha hailed from Benaras, reflecting their historical roots.
        - Q67: Consider the following statements:
            - Statements:
                1. The last Mauryan ruler, Brihadratha was assassinated by his commander-in-chief, Pushyamitra Sunga
                2. The last Sunga king, Devabhuti was assassinated by his Brahmana Minister Vasudeva Kanva who usurped the throne
                3. The last ruler of the Kanva dynasty was deposed by the Andhras
            - Options:
                (a) 1 and 2
                (b) Only 2
                (c) Only 3
                (d) 1, 2 and 3
            - Answer:
                (d) 1, 2 and 3
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - This question traces the decline of three successive dynasties in ancient India: the Mauryas (c. 321-185 BCE), Sungas (c. 185-73 BCE), and Kanvas (c. 73-28 BCE), culminating in the rise of the Andhras (Satavahanas), centered in Magadha (modern Bihar).
                - Evaluation of Statements:
                    - 1. The last Mauryan ruler, Brihadratha was assassinated by his commander-in-chief, Pushyamitra Sunga:
                        - True—Brihadratha, the final Mauryan king (r. c. 187-185 BCE), was killed by Pushyamitra Sunga, his general, in 185 BCE during a military review in Pataliputra (Patna, Bihar). This ended Mauryan rule, as per the Harshacharita and Puranas.
                    - 2. The last Sunga king, Devabhuti was assassinated by his Brahmana Minister Vasudeva Kanva who usurped the throne:
                        - True—Devabhuti (or Devabhumi), the last Sunga ruler (r. c. 83-73 BCE), was murdered by his minister Vasudeva Kanva around 73 BCE, who then founded the Kanva dynasty. This is noted in the Vishnu Purana and Bana’s Harshacharita.
                    - 3. The last ruler of the Kanva dynasty was deposed by the Andhras:
                        - True—The last Kanva king, Susharman (r. c. 38-28 BCE), was overthrown by Simuka, founder of the Andhra (Satavahana) dynasty, around 28 BCE, marking the end of Kanva rule, as per the Matsya Purana and archaeological evidence (e.g., Nasik inscriptions).
                - Why All Are Correct?:
                    - Statement 1:
                        - Pushyamitra’s coup in 185 BCE shifted power from the Mauryas to the Sungas, ending Ashoka’s lineage.
                    - Statement 2:
                        - Vasudeva’s assassination of Devabhuti in 73 BCE reflects internal betrayal, a pattern in dynastic transitions.
                    - Statement 3:
                        - The Andhras’ rise c. 28 BCE displaced the Kanvas, establishing Satavahana dominance in the Deccan and Magadha.
                - Dynastic Transitions:
                    - Mauryas to Sungas:
                        - 185 BCE, assassination of Brihadratha by Pushyamitra (Pataliputra, Bihar).
                    - Sungas to Kanvas:
                        - 73 BCE, Vasudeva kills Devabhuti, brief Kanva rule begins.
                    - Kanvas to Andhras:
                        - 28 BCE, Simuka deposes Susharman, Satavahanas rise (Pratishthana, Maharashtra).
                - Related Figures:
                    - Brihadratha:
                        - Last Mauryan king (r. c. 187-185 BCE), killed by Pushyamitra.
                    - Pushyamitra Sunga:
                        - Sunga founder (r. 185-149 BCE), overthrew Mauryas (e.g., Q34 [1998] context).
                    - Vasudeva Kanva:
                        - Kanva founder (r. 73-64 BCE), assassinated Devabhuti.
                    - Simuka:
                        - Andhra (Satavahana) founder (r. c. 28 BCE), ended Kanva rule.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to Mauryan decline (e.g., Q34 [1998]), Sunga rule (e.g., Q42 [1998] context), or Satavahana rise (e.g., Q41 [1998] trade).
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - All statements are correct: Brihadratha’s assassination by Pushyamitra (1), Devabhuti’s murder by Vasudeva (2), and the Kanvas’ fall to the Andhras (3) mark successive dynastic upheavals in ancient India.
